The Section 5 DocRED files contain a comprehensive experiments section with:

1. **Table: Four-Way Ablation on DocRED** (Table~\ref{tab:docred-ablation})

```text
1	% Section 5 — DocRED Results (Draft)
2	% Data source: Phase 2 DocRED registry experiments (checker_version: fixed_20260514)
3	% exp_ids: phase2_docred_unconstrained, phase2_docred_grammar_only,
4	%          phase2_docred_semantic_only, phase2_docred_full_s1
5	% All numbers verified against registry 2026-05-14.
6	% F1 computed via all_set method with name→Wikidata PID normalization.
7	
8	\subsection{Cross-Dataset Validation on DocRED}
9	\label{sec:docred-results}
10	
11	Table~\ref{tab:docred-ablation} presents the four-way ablation on DocRED,
12	a document-level relation extraction benchmark with 96 Wikidata relation
13	types---14$\times$ the ontology size of SciERC
14	(Section~\ref{sec:scierc-results}).
15	All experiments use Qwen3-8B in a zero-shot setting on 988 test documents
16	with identical prompts and decoding parameters.
17	Relation F1 is computed via name-to-Wikidata-PID normalization:
18	each predicted relation name is mapped to its canonical PID before
19	matching against gold annotations, and predictions that cannot be mapped
20	to any valid PID are counted as false positives.
21	
22	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
23	%  Table: DocRED four-way ablation
24	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
25	\begin{table}[t]
26	\centering
27	\small
28	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.6pt}
29	\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrr rr@{}}
30	\toprule
31	& \multicolumn{5}{c}{\textbf{Violation Rates (\%)}}
32	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{F1}} \\
33	\cmidrule(lr){2-6} \cmidrule(l){7-8}
34	\textbf{Mode}
35	  & DR\,$\downarrow$ & $\Delta$DR
36	  & RV\,$\downarrow$ & ET\,$\downarrow$ & CF
37	  & Rel.\,$\uparrow$ & Ent.\,$\uparrow$ \\
38	\midrule
39	Unconstrained
40	  & 18.46 & ---
41	  & 29.66 & 8.18
42	  & 0.10
43	  & 7.74  & 58.15 \\
44	Grammar
45	  & 26.17 & $\uparrow$42\%
46	  & \textbf{0.03} & \textbf{0}
47	  & 0.07
48	  & \textbf{8.31}  & --- \\
49	Semantic
50	  & 13.84 & $\downarrow$25\%
51	  & 29.87 & 10.44
52	  & 1.05
53	  & 7.70  & 57.81 \\
54	Full (S1)
55	  & \textbf{10.19} & $\downarrow$\textbf{45\%}
56	  & \textbf{0.03} & \textbf{0}
57	  & 2.57
58	  & 8.23  & \textbf{59.37} \\
59	\bottomrule
60	\end{tabular}
61	\caption{Four-way ablation on DocRED (988 documents, Qwen3-8B, zero-shot).
62	DR: domain/range violation rate;
63	RV: relation vocabulary violation rate;
64	ET: entity type violation rate;
65	CF: cross-field consistency violation rate.
66	$\Delta$DR: relative change vs.\ the unconstrained baseline
67	($\uparrow$: increase; $\downarrow$: decrease).
68	Best per column in \textbf{bold} (CF excluded---see text).
69	F1 scores are multiplied by 100.
70	Grammar-only entity F1 is unavailable (---).}
71	\label{tab:docred-ablation}
72	\end{table}
73	
74	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
75	%  (a) Core result
76	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
77	\paragraph{Core result: DR halved at zero F1 cost.}
78	The full two-layer configuration reduces domain/range violations from
79	18.46\% to 10.19\%, a 44.8\% relative reduction, while simultaneously
80	eliminating entity type violations (8.18\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and
81	near-eliminating relation vocabulary violations
82	(29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).
83	% phase2_docred_unconstrained: DR=0.1846, ET=0.0818, RV=0.2966
84	% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019, ET=0, RV=0.0003
85	Relation F1 moves from 7.74 to 8.23 (+0.49\,pp) and entity F1 from
86	58.15 to 59.37 (+1.22\,pp);
87	% phase2_docred_unconstrained: R-F1=0.0774, E-F1=0.5815
88	% phase2_docred_full_s1: R-F1=0.0823, E-F1=0.5937
89	as with SciERC, these differences are within noise for single-seed
90	evaluation and are best characterized as F1-neutral.
91	Cross-field consistency violations increase modestly from 0.10\% to
92	2.57\%, substantially lower than the 9.90\% observed on SciERC;
93	% phase2_docred_unconstrained: CF=0.0010; phase2_docred_full_s1: CF=0.0257
94	we discuss this trade-off in Section~\ref{sec:discussion}.
95	
96	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
97	%  (b) Grammar layer
98	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
99	\paragraph{Grammar layer: structural guarantees generalize, but surface latent violations.}
100	Grammar-only constraints generalize to DocRED's 96-relation ontology,
101	eliminating entity type violations (8.18\%\,$\to$\,0\%) and
102	near-eliminating relation vocabulary violations
103	(29.66\%\,$\to$\,0.03\%).
104	% phase2_docred_grammar_only: ET=0, RV=0.0003
105	However, domain/range violations \emph{increase} from 18.46\% to
106	26.17\% (+42\% relative).
107	% phase2_docred_grammar_only: DR=0.2617
108	This counter-intuitive result has a structural explanation.
109	In the unconstrained baseline, 29.66\% of triples contain
110	out-of-vocabulary relation names and are classified as RV violations;
111	because no schema entry exists for an invalid relation name,
112	these triples \emph{cannot} be evaluated against domain/range rules
113	and are therefore absent from the DR count.
114	When grammar constraints force all relation names into the valid
115	vocabulary, these formerly RV-classified triples receive valid
116	relation names---but the associated entity types frequently violate
117	the domain/range rules of the assigned relation, surfacing as new
118	DR violations.
119	The grammar layer thus \emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities
120	rather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:
121	without domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type
122	violation landscape worse, not better.
123	
124	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
125	%  (c) Enabling dependency
126	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
127	\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}
128	Semantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\%
129	(18.46\%\,$\to$\,13.84\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary
130	violations (RV\,=\,29.87\%) or entity type violations
131	(ET\,=\,10.44\%).
132	% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044
133	The elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\% vs.\ 8.18\%
134	unconstrained) is a side effect of the
135	\textsc{TypeConstraintProcessor}'s logit masking, which occasionally
136	introduces leading spaces in entity type tokens; we address this
137	artifact in the limitations.
138	Combining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\% relative to
139	the semantic-only configuration (13.84\%\,$\to$\,10.19\%),
140	% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264
141	replicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:
142	without grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer
143	encounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range
144	rules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.
145	The consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially
146	different ontology sizes (7 vs.\ 96 relation types) and violation
147	profiles (SciERC RV\,=\,1.39\% vs.\ DocRED RV\,=\,29.66\%) confirms
148	that the grammar$\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general
149	architectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a
150	particular schema.
151	
152	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
153	%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling
154	% ──────────────────────────────────────────
155	\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}
156	The residual DR on DocRED (10.19\%) substantially exceeds that of
157	SciERC (0.93\%, Section~\ref{sec:scierc-results}).
158	This gap is explained by per-relation \emph{constraint tightness}:
159	DocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs
160	(mean tightness\,=\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\% of entity type pairs violate
161	domain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC
162	(tightness\,=\,0.333).
163	% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842
164	% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333
165	With tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask
166	eliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,
167	increasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and
168	semantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.
169	In the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,
170	preserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement
171	for that token position.
172	Conversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives
173	for the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling
174	near-complete DR elimination.
175	The DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:
176	45\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\ 94\% on SciERC (loose).
177	% DocRED: DR_reduction=44.8%; SciERC: DR_reduction=93.9%
178	This represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization
179	of how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement
180	effectiveness; we develop this analysis further in
181	Section~\ref{sec:discussion}.

Now I'm verifying these results against the registry data, starting with the unconstrained baseline and working through each constraint mode to ensure the reported metrics match what's actually recorded. Now I'm checking the semantic-only results, which show a notable drop in detection rate to 13.84% with a 25% relative decrease from baseline. Moving to the full S1 configuration, the detection rate falls further to 10.19%, representing a 45% relative decrease—though there's a minor discrepancy between what the paper states in the text versus the table.

Looking at the remaining metrics for Full S1, the relation and entity F1 scores improve slightly to 8.23 and 59.37 respectively, while other error categories remain minimal. I'm noticing the paper reports the relative reduction as 44.8% in the text but rounds it to 45% in the table, which is technically accurate but creates an inconsistency worth flagging. Continuing to verify the grammar layer calculations, I'm checking the relative improvement percentages and confirming that the 29.66% figure represents the unconstrained relation vocabulary rate. Moving into the dependency enabling section, I'm validating the dependency relation reductions—the 25% relative decrease from 18.46% to 13.84%, along with the vocabulary and entity type metrics, and confirming the subsequent 26% relative improvement when additional constraints are applied.

Now I'm working through the constraint tightness calculation for the grammar layer. I need to verify how tightness is computed—it should be 1 minus the ratio of valid entity type pairs to total possible pairs, and I'm trying to determine what the denominator should be based on DocRED's entity type inventory.

For SciERC with 7 entity types, I get 49 total possible pairs, which would give tightness = 0.531 for 23 valid pairs, but the reported value is 0.333. This suggests either the calculation uses a different formula, or tightness is computed per-relation and then averaged across relations. I'm working through different denominator possibilities—if there are 6 entity types that gives 36 possible pairs, or 7 types gives 49, but neither quite lands on the 0.333 tightness the paper reports. Let me reconsider whether the calculation is actually the mean tightness across all relations in SciERC rather than a single relation. Working through the math, I can verify the tightness calculations: DocRED's 5.68 valid pairs with 0.842 tightness implies about 36 total possible pairs, and SciERC's 23 valid pairs with 0.333 tightness also points to roughly 36 total pairs. Both datasets have around 6 entity types, which checks out—6×6 gives us that ~36 pair space, with SciERC allowing most combinations while DocRED is much more restrictive.

I notice there's a minor inconsistency in how the results are reported: the table shows "↓45%" for DocRED but the text says "44.8%", when the actual value is 44.79%. The text is more precise, so I should standardize on that. For SciERC, "94%" is a reasonable rounding of the actual 93.95%, so that's fine.

Looking at the narrative structure, the four paragraphs flow logically from the core result through the grammar layer explanation, then to why semantic constraints alone aren't sufficient, and finally to why DocRED's residual DR is higher than SciERC's. The progression makes sense.

But there's a problem with the explanation on line 133-137 about the elevated ET under semantic-only decoding. The claim about "leading spaces in entity type tokens" from the TypeConstraintProcessor's logit masking isn't actually supported by the evidence presented.

The registry data shows the ET increase comes from genuine non-standard entity types like WORK, EVENT, and ETHNIC_GROUP—not from space artifacts. The checker fix that strips spaces did remove 507 false positives, but after that fix, ET remains at 10.44%, still higher than the unconstrained 8.18%. So the remaining violations are real type violations, not space-related issues. The narrative is misattributing the cause here.

On line 167-171, the description of the fallback mechanism when grammar and semantic masks intersect to create a narrow or empty candidate set is accurate. But I need to check whether the actual fallback frequency data supports how often this actually happens in practice.

The novelty claim on line 178-179 about being the first to empirically characterize how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement effectiveness is strong. I can't find prior work that specifically measures how constraint tightness affects decoding-time enforcement DR reduction—PICARD constrains SQL without analyzing tightness, XGrammar constrains JSON without domain/range equivalents. The claim seems plausible but I can't fully verify it.

Now I'm looking at potential reviewer attack surfaces, starting with the concern that 10.19% DR is still quite high.

The title frames this honestly as "DR halved at zero F1 cost," but a reviewer will likely notice the stark difference between SciERC (94% reduction) and DocRED (45% reduction) and argue the method doesn't generalize well to complex ontologies. The constraint tightness paragraph does address this, though it's more descriptive than prescriptive—explaining how it scales rather than proposing solutions.

There's also a credibility risk around the semantic-only ET increase. If a reviewer checks the registry notes, they'll see the increase comes from real type violations like WORK, EVENT, and ETHNIC_GROUP, not spacing issues. This inconsistency could undermine trust in the analysis.

The F1 changes are small (+0.49pp, +1.22pp) and within noise for single-seed evaluation, which the paper correctly notes. But the DR changes are also single-seed, and the paper should either provide confidence intervals or explicitly flag this limitation.

The missing Grammar entity F1 value in the table needs explanation—it's null in the registry, so either the computation should be shown or the reason for the gap clarified. Similarly, the constraint tightness numbers lack derivation; a reviewer will want to see how those values were actually computed. And the "first empirical characterization" claim is risky if prior work on constraint tightness and decoding effectiveness exists.

Looking at the experiments section, the ablation covers all combinations and the analysis is solid, but there's a notable gap: no post-hoc filtering baseline is mentioned, which was flagged as a missing comparison. The core issues boil down to a factual error in explaining the semantic-only ET increase (it's not leading spaces but non-standard entity types), a rounding inconsistency with ΔDR (44.8% vs 45%), the unexplained Grammar F1 gap, and constraint tightness values presented without showing how they were derived.

I'm also noticing a data count discrepancy — the text claims 988 test documents, but the actual processing shows 986-988 documents checked across different conditions with 11-13 parse failures each, which doesn't quite align. The ET triple counting bug flagged in the roadmap could shift these numbers further, so this section might need to wait for that fix before finalizing. I'm noticing the discrepancy more clearly now — the text claims all experiments use 988 documents, but the actual documents_checked values vary by mode (986, 985, 987, 988) due to different parse failure rates. There's also confusion about whether the dataset size is 998 or 999, since the violation report shows total_documents as 999 while the spec indicates 998, possibly due to a header line or duplicate entry.

The fix is straightforward: the paper should reference "998 documents from the DocRED development set" with a note explaining that parse failures caused slight variations in the number of documents actually checked across different modes. Now I'm ready to compile the full review.

Claim 可信度 — 数字交叉验证\n\n**Table 数字** 全部与 registry 匹配 ✅：\n- Unconstrained: DR=18.46%, RV=29.66%, ET=8.18%, CF=0.10%, R-F1=7.74, E-F1=58.15 ✓\n- Grammar: DR=26.17%, RV=0.03%, ET=0%, CF=0.07%, R-F1=8.31 ✓\n- Semantic: DR=13.84%, RV=29.87%, ET=10.44%, CF=1.05%, R-F1=7.70, E-F1=57.81 ✓\n- Full S1: DR=10.19%, RV=0.03%, ET=0%, CF=2.57%, R-F1=8.23, E-F1=59.37 ✓\n\n---\n\n### B. 具体问题\n\n**1. [severity: critical] Semantic-only ET 上升原因归因错误（line 133-137）**\n\n论文写道：\"The elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44% vs. 8.18% unconstrained) is a side effect of the TypeConstraintProcessor's logit masking, which occasionally introduces **leading spaces in entity type tokens**\"。\n\n这与 registry 证据不符。`phase2_docred_semantic_only` notes 明确记录：\n- checker 修复（strip + PERSON alias）已将 ET 从 18.44% 降至 10.44%（strip 修 507 假阳性，alias 修 1108 假阳性）\n- **修复后剩余的 2107 violations 是\"真实退化\"**：WORK(823), EVENT(743), ETHNIC_GROUP(137) 等非标类型\n- 这不是 leading space artifact——space 问题已被 strip() 修正，残余 ET 高于 unconstrained 是因为 semantic masking 改变了生成轨迹，导致模型更多产出这些非标 entity types\n\n**审稿人攻击**：如果审稿人追溯到 checker notes，会发现 space 已被修正，paper 的解释与实际根因矛盾，直接质疑方法论理解是否正确。\n\n**建议修改**：改为 \"Semantic-only decoding shifts the generation trajectory, causing the model to produce more non-standard entity types (e.g., WORK, EVENT) that fall outside DocRED's canonical type vocabulary. This is a genuine side effect of constrained decoding rather than a measurement artifact; we discuss this in the limitations.\"\n\n**2. [severity: warning] 文档数 \"988 test documents\" 不准确（line 16）**\n\n论文称 \"All experiments use Qwen3-8B in a zero-shot setting on **988** test documents\"。实际情况：\n- DocRED dev set = 998 documents\n- 各模式的 documents_checked 不同：unconstrained 986, grammar 985, semantic 987, full_s1 988\n- \"988\" 仅是 full_s1 的成功解析数，不是数据集大小\n\n**建议**：改为 \"998 documents from the DocRED development set\"，在 table caption 或脚注注明 parse failure rates（1.0-1.3%）。\n\n**3. [severity: warning] ΔDR 数字不一致：44.8% vs 45%**\n\n- Line 79（正文）：\"a 44.8% relative reduction\" → 精确值 44.79%\n- Table ΔDR 列：\"↓**45%**\"\n- Line 176（tightness 段）：\"45% on DocRED\"\n\nTable 和 tightness 段用 45%，正文用 44.8%。对同一个数字用两种精度，审稿人会质疑是否在挑更好看的数字。**建议全文统一为 \"45%\" 或 \"44.8%\"，不要两个都用。**\n\n**4. [severity: warning] Grammar entity F1 缺失（\"---\"）未解释**\n\nTable 中 Grammar-only E-F1 显示 \"---\"，caption 只说 \"Grammar-only entity F1 is unavailable\"。但审稿人会问：为什么其他三个模式都有 E-F1，唯独 grammar-only 没有？是代码 bug？是有意省略？Registry 确实显示 `entity_f1: null`，但这需要一个合理的解释（可能是 grammar-only 的 entity 没有做 type 分类，或 checker 在这个模式下没跑 entity evaluation）。**不解释 = 审稿人会用这个打低分。**\n\n**5. [severity: warning] Constraint tightness 数值（5.68, 23, 0.842, 0.333）缺乏推导**\n\nLines 159-163 直接给出 tightness 数值但未说明计算方法。审稿人需要知道：(a) tightness 如何定义（1 - valid_pairs / total_possible_pairs?），(b) total_possible_pairs 是什么（entity_types² ?），(c) 这些数值是从 constraint 文件计算还是从实验观察。**建议在 Method 段给出形式化定义，此处引用即可。**\n\n**6. [severity: warning] Semantic fallback 频率未报告**\n\nTightness 段（lines 167-171）正确描述了 grammar-semantic 交集为空时的 fallback 机制，但未给出 fallback 频率。我此前审查已量化：Llama DocRED full_s1 有 32,921 次 fallback (577 docs, ~57 次/doc)，SciERC 仅 5,882 次 (~10.7/doc)。**这些数据直接支撑 tightness 论点**——\"in the limiting case\" 不是理论推测，而是每个 document 平均发生 57 次。不报告此数据是白白丢掉强证据。\n\n---\n\n### C. 叙事逻辑评估\n\n**段落逻辑链**：Core result → Grammar explanation → Enabling dependency → Tightness scaling。逻辑清晰，每段递进。✓\n\n**Grammar DR 上升的解释**（lines 99-122）质量很高——\"reveals latent type incompatibilities rather than creating them\" 是好的 framing，能预防审稿人 \"your method makes things worse\" 的攻击。✓\n\n**\"first empirical characterization\"**（line 178-179）是强 novelty claim。建议 Agent 用 WebSearch 验证是否有 prior work 量化 constraint tightness vs decoding enforcement effectiveness。此 claim 如被推翻，损失很大。\n\n---\n\n### D. 审稿人攻击面\n\n1. **\"10% DR 哪里 near-complete 了？\"** — tightness 段有解释但不提供解决方案。审稿人会要求 \"then what's the fix for tight schemas?\" 建议加一句 future work（adaptive threshold / constraint relaxation）。\n2. **Post-hoc filtering 未对比** — Dream 已识别此缺口，ROADMAP 有计划。但 §5 应预留对比行或脚注说明。\n3. **单 seed，无 CI** — 正文说 \"within noise for single-seed evaluation\"（line 89），诚实。但审稿人可能要求 bootstrap CI 也包括 violation rate，不仅是 F1。\n\n---\n\n### E. 待定风险\n\nROADMAP 标注的 **ET 三重计数 bug** 如果尚未修正，Table 中 ET 数字可能不准确。当前 ET=8.18% (unconstrained) 和 ET=10.44% (semantic) 如果被 inflate，修正后差异可能变小。**建议先修 checker、统一所有数字后再定稿此 section。**\n\n---\n\n### 总结\n\n数字与 registry 一致（✓），叙事逻辑清晰（✓），constraint tightness 分析有独立 insight 价值（✓）。但 **ET 归因错误是 critical**——将已被 checker 修正的 space artifact 当作当前 ET 上升的原因，直接与 registry notes 矛盾。Grammar E-F1 缺失和 tightness 数值缺推导是较易修正的 warning。建议修正后再提交 review cycle。",
  "project": "schema_semantic_constrained_ie"
}
```

F1 method: all_set (OOV predictions included in denominator). 之前 recognized_set 方法 (R-F1=9.33%→8.17%, -1.16pp) 是错误的——它过滤了 unconstrained 的 5189 个 OOV predictions，人为抬高 precision。Grammar-only DR 上升(18.46→26.17%)是审稿必问点：grammar 强制 valid relation name 后 entity type 与新 relation 的 domain/range 不匹配加剧，恰好证明 semantic 层必要性。\", \"completed_at\": \"2026-05-14T02:55:47.271Z\", \"extra\": {"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"\\\"\\\"\\\"Post-hoc estimation of semantic fallback frequency for Qwen outputs.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\nimport json\\nfrom collections import defaultdict\\nfrom pathlib import Path\\n\\n\\n# === DocRED entity type aliases (from violation_checker_docred.py) ===\\nDOCRED_TYPE_ALIASES = {\\n    'PERSON': 'PER', 'PEOPLE': 'PER',\\n    'ORGANIZATION': 'ORG', 'ORGANISATION': 'ORG',\\n    'LOCATION': 'LOC', 'NUMBER': 'NUM', 'NUMERICAL': 'NUM',\\n    'MISCELLANEOUS': 'MISC',\\n}\\n\\n# === SciERC entity type aliases (from violation_checker.py) ===\\nSCIERC_TYPE_MAP = {\\n    \\\"otherscientificterm\\\": \\\"OtherScientificTerm\\\",\\n    \\\"other-scientificterm\\\": \\\"OtherScientificTerm\\\",\\n    \\\"other_scientific_term\\\": \\\"OtherScientificTerm\\\",\\n    \\\"scientificterm\\\": \\\"OtherScientificTerm\\\",\\n    \\\"other\\\": \\\"OtherScientificTerm\\\",\\n}\\n\\n\\ndef normalize_docred_type(raw):\\n    t = raw.strip().upper()\\n    return DOCRED_TYPE_ALIASES.get(t, t)\\n\\n\\ndef normalize_scierc_type(raw, valid_types):\\n    t_lower = raw.strip().lower().replace(\\\" \\\", \\\"\\\").replace(\\\"_\\\", \\\"\\\").replace(\\\"-\\\", \\\"\\\")\\n    for vt in valid_types:\\n        if t_lower == vt.lower().replace(\\\" \\\", \\\"\\\").replace(\\\"_\\\", \\\"\\\").replace(\\\"-\\\", \\\"\\\"):\\n            return vt\\n    return SCIERC_TYPE_MAP.get(t_lower, None)\\n\\n\\ndef build_docred_name_to_id(rel_types):\\n    m = {}\\n    for rid, rname in rel_types.items():\\n        m[rname.lower().strip()] = rid\\n        m[rid.lower()] = rid\\n    return m\\n\\n\\ndef estimate_docred(output_path, constraints_path):\\n    with open(constraints_path) as f:\\n        C = json.load(f)\\n\\n    rel_types = C[\\\"relation_types\\\"]\\n    rel_constraints = C[\\\"relation_constraints\\\"]\\n    name_to_id = build_docred_name_to_id(rel_types)\\n\\n    # Build valid_pairs sets keyed by PID\\n    valid_pairs = {}\\n    for pid, info in rel_constraints.items():\\n        valid_pairs[pid] = set(tuple(p) for p in info[\\\"valid_pairs\\\"])\\n\\n    total_triples = 0\\n    fallback_triples = 0\\n    n_docs = 0\\n    unmatched_relations = defaultdict(int)\\n    per_rel_stats = defaultdict(lambda: {\\\"total\\\": 0, \\\"fallback\\\": 0})\\n    fallback_examples = []\\n\\n    with open(output_path) as f:\\n        for line in f:\\n            doc = json.loads(line)\\n            n_docs += 1\\n            parsed = doc.get(\\\"parsed_output\\\") or {}\\n            rels = parsed.get(\\\"relations\\\", []) if parsed else []\\n\\n            for r in rels:\\n                total_triples += 1\\n                rel_name = r.get(\\\"type\\\", \\\"\\\")\\n                ht = normalize_docred_type(r.get(\\\"head_type\\\", \\\"\\\"))\\n                tt = normalize_docred_type(r.get(\\\"tail_type\\\", \\\"\\\"))\\n\\n                pid = name_to_id.get(rel_name.lower().strip())\\n                if pid is None:\\n                    # The project targets EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25 submission with internal draft deadline May 19.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **OntoCoDec**: Two-layer constrained decoding — Grammar (XGrammar JSON schema + enum) + Semantic (TypeConstraintProcessor with entity registry + logit masking)
   - **Violation types**: DR (domain/range), RV (relation vocabulary), ET (entity type), CF (cross-field consistency)
   - **S1 vs S2 strategies**: S1 single-pass (both layers throughout), S2 two-pass (grammar first, then semantic). S1 is primary; S2 demoted to ablation.
   - **Semantic fallback**: When grammar-valid and semantically-valid token sets have empty intersection, system falls back to grammar-only — key factor in DocRED's residual DR
   - **Constraint tightness**: DocRED mean tightness=0.842 (5.68 valid pairs/relation), SciERC=0.333 (23 valid pairs). Tighter constraints → more fallbacks → higher residual DR
   - **F1 method**: all_set (includes OOV predictions in denominator) is the correct/fair method; recognized_set artificially inflates precision
   - **Checker fixes**: strip() + PERSON→PER alias normalization applied; ET triple counting bug still pending
   - **Dream analysis**: Simulated review scoring 3/6 across all scenarios, identifying 6 structural gaps

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **Registry (registry.yaml)**: 19 experiments total (16 success, 1 killed, 2 RUNNING). Contains all metrics, specs, conclusions. Read multiple times to verify numbers.

   **Critical insight discovered**: Qwen DocRED fallback_triples=1770 almost exactly matches the DR violations=1771 from the violation report. This confirms fallback≈DR violations — when semantic masking falls back, the resulting triple almost always has a domain/range violation.

assigned relation, surfacing as new\nDR violations.\nThe grammar layer thus \\emph{reveals} latent type incompatibilities\nrather than creating them, directly motivating the semantic layer:\nwithout domain/range enforcement, grammar alone makes the type\nviolation landscape worse, not better.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (c) Enabling dependency\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Enabling dependency confirmed across datasets.}\nSemantic constraints alone reduce DR by 25\\%\n(18.46\\%\\,$\\to$\\,13.84\\%) but cannot eliminate relation vocabulary\nviolations (RV\\,=\\,29.87\\%) or entity type violations\n(ET\\,=\\,10.44\\%).\n% phase2_docred_semantic_only: DR=0.1384, RV=0.2987, ET=0.1044\nThe elevated ET under semantic-only decoding (10.44\\% vs.\\ 8.18\\%\nunconstrained) reflects a genuine trajectory perturbation:\nconstrained logit masking shifts the generation distribution,\ncausing the model to produce more non-standard entity types\n(e.g., \\textsc{Work}, \\textsc{Event}, \\textsc{Ethnic\\_Group})\nthat fall outside DocRED's canonical six-type vocabulary.\n% Top non-standard types: WORK (823), EVENT (743), ETHNIC_GROUP (137)\nWe discuss this side effect in the limitations.\nCombining both layers (Full S1) reduces DR a further 26\\% relative to\nthe semantic-only configuration (13.84\\%\\,$\\to$\\,10.19\\%),\n% phase2_docred_full_s1: DR=0.1019; delta=(13.84-10.19)/13.84=0.264\nreplicating the enabling dependency observed on SciERC:\nwithout grammar-enforced vocabulary constraints, the semantic layer\nencounters out-of-vocabulary relation types for which no domain/range\nrules exist, limiting its enforcement capacity.\nThe consistency of this pattern across two datasets with substantially\ndifferent ontology sizes (7 vs.\\ 96 relation types) and violation\nprofiles (SciERC RV\\,=\\,1.39\\% vs.\\ DocRED RV\\,=\\,29.66\\%) confirms\nthat the grammar$\\to$semantic enabling dependency is a general\narchitectural property of the framework, not an artifact of a\nparticular schema.\n\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n%  (d) Constraint tightness scaling\n% ──────────────────────────────────────────\n\\paragraph{Constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling.}\nThe residual DR on DocRED (10.19\\%) substantially exceeds that of\nSciERC (0.93\\%, Section~\\ref{sec:scierc-results}).\nThis gap is explained by per-relation \\emph{constraint tightness}:\nDocRED relations admit on average only 5.68 valid entity type pairs\n(mean tightness\\,=\\,0.842, i.e., 84.2\\% of entity type pairs violate\ndomain/range rules), compared to 23 valid pairs on SciERC\n(tightness\\,=\\,0.333).\n% DocRED: avg_valid_pairs=5.68, avg_tightness=0.842\n% SciERC: avg_valid_pairs=23, avg_tightness=0.333\nWith tighter per-relation constraints, the semantic layer's logit mask\neliminates a larger fraction of candidate tokens at each decoding step,\nincreasing the probability that the intersection of grammar and\nsemantic masks yields a narrow or empty candidate set.\nIn the limiting case, the system falls back to grammar-only constraints,\npreserving structural validity but forgoing domain/range enforcement\nfor that token position.\nConversely, SciERC's looser constraints leave more valid alternatives\nfor the model to redirect probability mass toward, enabling\nnear-complete DR elimination.\nThe DR reduction thus scales inversely with constraint tightness:\n45\\% on DocRED (tight) vs.\\ 94\\% on SciERC (loose).\n% DocRED: DR_reduction=45%; SciERC: DR_reduction=94%\nThis represents, to our knowledge, the first empirical characterization\nof how ontology constraint properties modulate decoding-time enforcement\neffectiveness; we develop this analysis further in\nSection~\\ref{sec:discussion}.\n",
        "numLines": 187,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 187
      }
    },

The key question: why does the estimation show 17341 total_triples but the registry says 17372?

Let me check what the estimation script actually reads. Looking at the script, it opens the output file and counts triples. The violation checker may count differently. The 31-triple difference could be:
1. The script excludes 31 triples for some reason (e.g., unmatched relations, parse issues)


2. The script loaded the wrong file

The script reports `matched_triples: 17336` and `unmatched_relations: {"membericipant of": 5}`, so 17336 + 5 = 17341 total. That's a 31-triple gap from the registry's 17372. I need to verify which file the script actually loaded for "full_s1" in its main block.

Looking at the actual output, I see 17341 total triples with 17336 matched, leaving just 5 unmatched triples that are all labeled as "membericipant of"—

a typo. But here's the puzzle: the violation checker reports 17372 triples across 988 documents, while the estimation script found 17341 triples in 998 documents. More documents but fewer triples doesn't add up unless "documents_checked" in the registry means something different than I'm interpreting—maybe it's only counting documents with valid output, not all documents attempted.

Looking at the registry more carefully, it shows documents_checked=988 and parse_failures=11, which suggests 977 documents actually have clean output. The 31-triple discrepancy between the two counts likely comes down to how strictly each script defines what constitutes a valid triple. But the key finding holds regardless: the fallback count of 1770 matches the expected value from the DR correlation almost perfectly, so this minor inconsistency doesn't undermine the main result.

Now I'm organizing the key points for my review. The post-hoc estimation approach is methodologically sound since checking output triples against ontology constraints serves as a valid proxy for semantic fallback behavior, even though it operates at a different granularity than Llama's token-level tracking. The core finding is striking—1770 fallback triples versus 17372 × 0.1019 ≈ 1770—which strongly supports the mechanistic hypothesis. The 31-triple gap between the estimation file and registry likely stems from the output file being regenerated after the violation checker ran. I should also validate this against the SciERC cross-validation results.

Now I'm checking the SciERC numbers more carefully. The registry shows a domain_range_violation_rate of 0.0008 for the Llama SciERC full_s1 experiment, but my estimation is reporting a fallback_rate of 0.0093—these don't align. I need to verify which Qwen SciERC full_s1 experiment corresponds to the estimation file being used. The fallback rate matches the DR rate perfectly for Qwen SciERC too, which confirms this pattern holds across both datasets. The high-fallback relations like P1198 (83%) and P171 (78%) are worth highlighting in the results section since they show which constraints are tightest. I should report both the Qwen triple-level violations and Llama token-level fallback frequencies since they capture different aspects — what constraints were actually broken versus how often the decoder resorted to fallback during generation. I'm also noticing the model generates "membericipant of" as a misspelling of "participant of" (P1344) in a few cases.

The grammar constraints should prevent invalid relation names entirely since they restrict the enum to valid relations, so finding 5 instances of "membericipant of" is puzzling — either the constraints weren't applied when that output was generated, the file got regenerated without them, or there's a parsing edge case that slipped through. The fact that both the estimation and registry report exactly 5 unrecognized relations confirms these are real violations in the output despite the grammar constraints supposedly being in place.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the critical data:

**Qwen SciERC full_s1 (phase2_scierc_full_s1)**:
- total_triples: 1729
- domain_range_violation_rate: 0.0093 (0.93%)
- DR violations = 1729 × 0.0093 = 16.08 ≈ 16

**Fallback estimation for Qwen SciERC full_s1**:
- total_triples: 1729 ✅ (matches!)


- fallback_triples: 16 ✅ (matches!)
- fallback_rate: 0.0093 ✅ (matches!)

The SciERC numbers align perfectly—the fallback triple count of 16 corresponds exactly to the domain-range violations. Now looking at DocRED, I'm seeing total_triples of 17372 in the registry with a violation rate of 0.1019, which gives roughly 1770 violations, but the fallback estimation shows 17341 triples instead.

That 31-triple gap is interesting—it matches the grammar_only total exactly, which seems like more than coincidence. I'm also noticing a timing issue: the violation checker ran on May 14 at 22:50, but the docred_full_s1 file was modified later on May 15 at 02:18, meaning the output file was rewritten after the violations were already computed. There's also a discrepancy in the document counts between the estimation script and the registry that I need to investigate.

The real takeaway though is that the fallback_triples metric aligns almost perfectly with the DR violations across both datasets, which validates the core finding despite these minor inconsistencies. The mechanistic explanation is clear: when semantic masking falls back to grammar-only matching, the resulting triples almost certainly violate domain/range constraints since the fallback happens precisely when valid type pairs run out. So fallback and DR violation are essentially the same thing, not just correlated.

I'm noting a few issues for the review. There's a minor discrepancy in the DocRED triple counts between the estimation script and violation checker—31 triples difference, likely because the estimation script loaded a file that was regenerated after the violation checker ran. The methodology itself checks out though: the exact per-relation rate matches on SciERC confirm that the estimation correctly identifies fallback triples as DR-violating triples.

The mechanistic insight that fallback maps to DR violation strengthens the constraint tightness analysis. I should incorporate the per-relation fallback rates into section 5 as concrete examples of how tight these constraints are. Also, the Llama and Qwen comparisons operate at different granularities—token-level versus triple-level—so both should be reported as complementary findings.

SciERC: 16 fallback triples / 1729 total = 0.93%，registry DR=0.0093 → 1729×0.0093=16.08 ✅\n  - DocRED: 1770 fallback triples，registry DR=0.1019 × 17372=1770.1 ✅\n  \n  机制解释：semantic masking fallback 发生时，该 triple 的 entity type pair 必然不在 valid_pairs 内（否则不会 fallback），因此输出 triple 必然是 DR violation。这不仅是相关性，而是逻辑必然。\n\n- [severity: warning] **DocRED triple 总数不一致**：estimation 报告 total_triples=17341，但 registry phase2_docred_full_s1 记录 total_triples=17372（差 31 个）。17341 恰好等于 phase2_docred_grammar_only 的 total_triples。原因：`docred_full_s1.jsonl` 文件时间戳为 May 15 02:18，**晚于** violation checker 运行时间 May 14 22:50，说明文件在两次分析之间被重写/修改。estimation 和 violation checker 可能跑在不同版本的输出文件上。差异仅 0.18%、不影响核心结论，但需要 Agent 确认文件是否被 post-hoc pipeline 覆写，并确保论文引用数字一致。\n\n- [severity: info] **Per-relation fallback 分布提供 §5 约束紧度的具体例证**：\n  - 高 fallback：P1198 (unemployment rate) 83%, P171 (parent taxon) 78%, P1336 (territory claimed by) 45%, P1056 (product/material produced) 31%\n  - 低/零 fallback：P127 (owned by) 0%, P159 (headquarters) 0%, P170 (creator) 0%, P175 (performer) 0%\n  \n  高 fallback 关系的 valid_pairs 极少（如 P1198 只有 6 个 triple，5 个 fallback），直接支撑 §5 \"constraint tightness governs enforcement ceiling\" 的论点。建议在 §5 或 Discussion 中加入 2-3 个具体关系作为 case study。\n\n- [severity: info] **Llama vs Qwen 数据互补**：Llama token-level（~57 fallback events/doc on DocRED）vs Qwen triple-level（1.77 fallback triples/doc）量纲不同——一个 triple 生成过程中可能触发多次 token-level fallback。两者描述同一现象的不同粒度，论文中应明确区分并同时报告。\n\n### 建议\n\n- 确认 `docred_full_s1.jsonl` 是否被 post-hoc pipeline 覆写。如是，需要对齐 violation checker 和 fallback estimation 使用的数据源。\n- 在 §5 constraint tightness 段落加入具体 per-relation fallback 数据（如 P1198=83% vs P127=0%），比仅引用 mean tightness=0.842 更有说服力。",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me carefully analyze the bootstrap script:

1. **Methodology**: 
   - 10,000 bootstrap resamples, percentile CI, seed=42
   - Resampling unit: sentence (SciERC) / document (DocRED) — this is correct
   - Paired bootstrap for comparisons — this is the right approach

2. **F1 computation**:
   The `bootstrap_f1` function computes micro-F1 (aggregates TP, FP, FN across all samples, then computes F1). The `point_estimate` is the micro-F1 on the full unresampled dataset.
   
3. **Entity and relation matching**:
   For SciERC, entities are matched as normalized text-type pairs, and relations use head-tail-type tuples for exact matching. DocRED follows a similar pattern based on the code structure, though the full implementation was cut off.

4. **Relation normalization differences**:
   SciERC uses relation names directly since they're already canonical, but DocRED has a critical distinction: gold annotations use PIDs (like P17, P127) while predicted outputs use relation names (country, owned by). The bootstrap script applies `docred_normalize_relation` to map predicted names to PIDs before matching against gold. I need to verify this by examining the `docred_sample_stats` function more closely.

5. **Entity handling as the likely culprit**:
   The registry's F1 computation might treat entities differently than the bootstrap script—particularly for DocRED where entities can span multiple mentions across a document. Gold annotations might reference entities by their canonical name while predictions are individual mentions, which could explain the matching discrepancies I'm seeing.

6. **Key findings to highlight**:
   The unconstrained vs. full_s1 comparison shows significant improvements in both entity and relation F1 for DocRED (entity F1 +0.53pp, relation F1 +0.50pp, both p<0.001), though there's a notable discrepancy between bootstrap and registry calculations that I need to investigate further, plus the grammar_only entity F1 issue that needs flagging.

For SciERC, the same comparison yields no significant differences (entity F1 -0.03pp p=0.527, relation F1 +0.13pp p=0.582), but the unconstrained vs. grammar_only comparison for DocRED shows strong significance again (entity F1 +0.47pp p=0.0008, relation F1 +0.57pp p<0.001), which is actually encouraging for the overall narrative.

Looking more closely at the DocRED results, I notice something important: the semantic layer alone doesn't move the needle on F1 (unconstrained vs. semantic_only shows +0.03pp entity F1 p=0.7378 and -0.01pp relation F1 p=0.895), but adding the grammar layer does (semantic_only vs. full_s1 shows +0.50pp for both p<0.001). So the F1 gains are entirely driven by the grammar constraints, which makes sense since they enforce structural validity.

Comparing grammar_only to full_s1 directly, they're essentially equivalent on F1 (grammar_only is +0.06pp entity F1 p=0.224 and -0.07pp relation F1 p=0.138), confirming that semantic constraints don't add F1 value beyond what grammar provides. The mechanism is clear: grammar fixes entity type validity, which improves entity matching and cascades to relations, while semantic constraints just reduce violations without changing the entity types themselves.

This raises a question about why grammar_only's entity F1 is marked as "---" in the paper when the bootstrap actually shows it at 58.99 on DocRED—that's a meaningful number that directly demonstrates the mechanism.

Now I'll draft the review.
